  agent step 1: content: Both candidates are the same molecule, so there is nothing to cross over and the whole episode is a mutation study on one scaffold - which suits it, because this is the most kinase-like starting point I have been given. A pyrazolo[3,4-d]pyrimidine core with a donor at atom 6 flanked by ring nitrogen 14, a phenyl, and a pyrrolidine, at QED 0.803 and SA 2.16. It scores 1.684, third in the pool, and its jnk3 is still only 0.01.

The brief is specific about why that can happen. The donor requirement is not merely that an NH exists with an aryl somewhere beyond it - the aryl has to sit directly on the donor nitrogen. Here it does not: atom 7 is a CH2 between the nitrogen and the ring, making this a benzylamine rather than an anilino group. That single sp3 carbon breaks conjugation between the phenyl and the donor and lets the ring rotate out of the plane of the core, and coplanarity is the third element the brief ranks.

So: replace_substructure at the bond from atom 6 (the N-H, kept) to atom 7 (the CH2, discarded along with its phenyl), attaching a fresh phenyl in its place. Net effect is the deletion of exactly one carbon - benzylamino becomes anilino - which makes this an unusually clean matched pair against a molecule already in the scored pool. Expect MW 308 to about 294, rotatable bonds 4 to 3, QED roughly flat, SA slightly better. If the binding term moves on a one-atom change, that is strong evidence for the brief's coplanarity claim; if it does not, the claim is weaker than stated and I would rather learn that here than on a molecule where five things changed at once.

  agent step 1: content: Ligand 1's QED is 0.890, the highest in the pool, and its SA is 3.48, the second worst - and one feature causes both halves of that. Atoms 5 and 8 are benzylic CH(CH3) centres, both stereodefined, bridged by a protonated secondary amine at atom 7. Two stereocentres plus a formal charge is what SA is pricing, and the amine is also why the molecule has a donor at all.

But it is the wrong kind of donor. The brief requires the aryl to sit directly on the donor nitrogen; here the nitrogen has a methyl-bearing sp3 carbon on each side, so neither ring is conjugated to it, and a charged aliphatic ammonium is not the neutral hinge donor the pharmacophore describes. Meanwhile atoms 2-4 and 16-19 are a 3-methoxy-4-fluorophenyl - a substituted aromatic carbocycle, which is exactly the kind of ring the brief wants kept.

So the move is to keep that ring and discard everything hanging off it. replace_substructure at the bond from atom 4 (the aromatic carbon, kept) to atom 5 (the stereocentre, discarded - taking the methyl, the ammonium, the second stereocentre and its phenyl with it), attaching Nc1ncccn1 in its place. Written that way the first atom is the exocyclic nitrogen, so it bonds through N and the result is an anilino group on pyrimidine C2: a neutral N-H with an aryl on one side and a ring nitrogen on the other, in one edit.

Expect MW 274 to about 220, both stereocentres and the charge gone, SA down from 3.48 toward 2, rotatable bonds 5 to 2. QED will likely slip from 0.890 since 220 Da is under its optimum, but I intend to spend the next edit putting that weight back as a pocket ring rather than as sp3 bulk. Ligand 2 I am leaving aside - its glycine ester adds two more rotatable bonds and its only donor is an amide NH with no adjacent ring nitrogen.

Across the three scored molecules with this architecture, the best is the fused oxindole at 0.58, and what distinguishes an oxindole from a dimethylphenyl or a methoxy-fluorophenyl is not size or lipophilicity - it is that the oxindole carries its own lactam N-H and carbonyl, a second donor/acceptor pair, on the donor-side ring. If that is what earns the 0.58, then a much simpler ring bearing a second donor should capture some of it. A para-amino group is the cheapest possible test of that idea.

  agent step 1: content: Self-paired, so this is a mutation study on the pool's third-best molecule, and the target is the only variable that has actually moved the binding term this run: the ring on the donor nitrogen. The scored series reads dimethylphenyl 0.47, methoxy-fluorophenyl 0.53, fused oxindole 0.58 - and this working molecule is the bottom of it.

What the oxindole has that the other two lack is a lactam: an N-H and a carbonyl on the donor-side ring. There are two quite different explanations for its lead. Either the fused bicycle itself is what the model recognises, or the amide is, and the fusion is incidental to it. Those point in opposite directions for the rest of the run - one says build harder-to-make fused systems, the other says a cheap acyclic substituent gets most of the benefit.

This edit separates them. replace_substructure at the bond from atom 7 (the donor N-H, kept) to atom 6 (the dimethylphenyl, discarded entirely), attaching 4-acetamidophenyl in its place. The first atom written is the ring carbon para to the acetamide, so the amide ends up directly opposite the donor. Now the corrected version of the same idea. What the donor-ring series rewards, reading across 0.47, 0.53 and 0.58, is substitution placed meta and para to the donor rather than ortho, made polar, and ideally tied back into a ring. What it punishes, as I just demonstrated at some cost, is adding a fourth aromatic ring. So it tests the meta-para ring-closure hypothesis while changing the electronics as little as possible, and it should be readable against both the parent at 0.53 and the oxindole at 0.58.

This one went to plan in every particular. QED 0.789 to 0.7985 - the highest of any molecule carrying this architecture all run, and above the parent rather than merely near it. SA 1.8547 to 1.9321, the small rise I expected from closing a ring. MW 295 to 291. LogP 4.0349 to 3.6159. Rotatable bonds 4 to 3, because tying the methoxy back into the ring removes its rotation. Three aromatic rings, as intended.

The visible sum is about 1.71 against the parent's 1.694, so unlike most of this run's experiments this one does not need the binding term to justify itself - it is already ahead on the readable objectives. If the binding term merely holds at the parent's 0.53 the molecule beats it outright; if the meta-para ring-closure reading of the 0.47 / 0.53 / 0.58 series is right and it lands nearer 0.58, it takes the top of the pool.

What I take from the episode beyond the molecule is the correction in the middle of it. I proposed the benzimidazolone as a one-atom change and it was not: replacing a lactam CH2 with N-H also aromatises the ring, and the fourth-aromatic-ring penalty cost more than the extra donor could return. The lesson generalises past this scaffold - when swapping an atom inside a ring, check whether the ring's aromaticity survives the swap before calling it a single variable. Getting that wrong cost me one modification; not undoing it would have cost an offspring. FINAL ANSWER

I was wrong twice here and both errors point the same way. I predicted SA would improve from 2.12 toward 1.9 because an indoline looked to me like a simpler building block than an oxindole; it came out at 2.1108, essentially unchanged. And I predicted QED would rise because MW drops from 302 to 288 and the carbonyl oxygen goes; instead it fell slightly, 0.778 to 0.7682, since 302 was already sitting on QED's optimum and moving away from it costs more than shedding an oxygen gains. The general mistake in both cases was assuming that removing an atom makes a molecule cheaper and more drug-like. Neither model works that way once you are already at a good size.

What that leaves is a tighter experiment than I planned. The visible sum is about 1.645 against the leader's 1.654 - within 0.01 - so the two molecules are nearly indistinguishable on the readable objectives and differ structurally in one thing: the lactam carbonyl is a CH2. Any real gap in their final scores is the carbonyl's contribution to binding, measured directly.

That pairs with the acetamide molecule elsewhere in this generation, which carries the N-H and carbonyl on an unfused ring. Between the two results the donor-ring question decomposes: if this one holds near 0.58 the carbonyl is dispensable and fusion is what matters; if it drops and the acetamide holds, the carbonyl matters and the ring does not; if both drop, the leader needs all three features together. I would rather have that decomposition than a fourth edit here. FINAL ANSWER

  agent step 1: content: The new scores force a correction, and it is a large one. I had been reading the donor-ring series as a trend - 0.47 for a dimethylphenyl, 0.53 for a methoxy-fluorophenyl, 0.58 for a fused oxindole - and extrapolating toward bigger, more polar, more fused rings. That reading was wrong, and it was wrong because I had no baseline. The unsubstituted reference compound I built specifically to calibrate the series scores 0.70, above every member of it. The 'trend' was three substituted rings all doing worse than plain phenyl, ranked among themselves.

With the baseline in place the real pattern is legible. Bare phenyl 0.70. Para-amino 0.75, para-acetamido 0.73 - both above bare. Ortho-and-meta dimethyl 0.47, and the three fused bicycles 0.58, 0.58, 0.59 - all well below. So substitution para to the donor is mildly rewarded, substitution ortho to it is heavily punished, and ring fusion is punished about as much regardless of what the fused ring carries. My whole benzodioxole and indoline line of work was chasing an artefact, and both molecules landed exactly where the fused penalty predicts once you know it exists.

Ligand 1 is one of those fused molecules. So: replace_substructure at the bond from atom 9 (the donor N-H, kept) to atom 10 (the benzodioxole, discarded), attaching 4-methoxyphenyl.

Para-methoxy is chosen to separate two explanations of the para effect. Amino gives 0.75 and acetamido 0.73; amino is both strongly electron-donating and a hydrogen-bond donor, acetamido is a donor but only weakly donating. Methoxy is strongly donating and not a donor at all. If it lands near 0.75 the effect is electronic; if near 0.70 it needs the N-H. Expect MW about 277, QED near 0.79, SA near 1.7.

  agent step 4: content: That is a decisive negative and it corrects a story I had been telling myself for two episodes. LogP behaved as predicted - 3.5928, the lowest of any molecule on this scaffold - and QED still collapsed to 0.7089 rather than rising to the 0.79 the LogP line extrapolated to.

The number that explains it is one I already had. The para-amino compound scored QED 0.709. This para-hydroxy compound scores 0.7089. An aniline and a phenol are chemically quite different - different pKa, different alert status, different LogP by half a unit - and they land on the same QED to four decimals. What they share is that both take the molecule from one hydrogen-bond donor to two.

So my explanation for the amino result was wrong. I attributed its low QED to the aniline structural alert and told myself a phenol would avoid it. The actual driver is donor count, and it costs roughly 0.07 on this scaffold regardless of which group supplies the donor. That also means my LogP line was fitted on five molecules that all happened to have exactly one donor, which is why it looked so clean and why it failed the moment I stepped outside that set. A monotonic fit across a variable I was not tracking.

Practically: every single-donor para substituent measured sits between 0.766 and 0.788, every two-donor one sits at 0.709, and the gap between those groups is larger than the whole spread within either. The rule for the rest of this run is to keep exactly one hydrogen-bond donor - the anilino N-H that the pharmacophore requires - and choose among single-donor substituents on LogP.

undo_last_change takes me back to the chlorine, which at QED 0.7657 and SA 1.6644 is the better molecule of the two by a clear margin.

  agent step 1: content: Ligand 1 has the highest binding term in the pool at 0.75, and it is held back by the weakest QED of any competitive molecule, 0.709. Fixing that without losing the binding is the most valuable single move available, and the para series now says fairly precisely what the QED problem is.

Among one-donor para substituents QED runs 0.766 to 0.788. The two molecules with a second donor at low weight - this amino at 262 Da and a hydroxy at 263 - both sit at 0.709, while the acetamido at 304 Da carries two donors and still reaches 0.768. So a second donor is affordable near QED's 300 Da optimum and expensive at 260. This molecule has the worst combination: a second donor and not enough mass.

replace_substructure at the bond from atom 1 (the ring carbon, kept) to atom 0 (the amino nitrogen, discarded), attaching N(C)C. That converts the primary amine into a dimethylamino - two methyls added, the N-H removed.

It fixes both halves at once. The molecule goes to about 290 Da, into the productive weight window, and back to a single hydrogen-bond donor, the anilino N-H the pharmacophore actually needs. And it should preserve whatever produced the 0.75, because a dimethylamino is if anything a stronger electron donor into the ring than a primary amine - if the para benefit is electronic, this keeps it; the group's steric bulk all points away from the donor nitrogen, at the far end of the ring, which is the position the series shows to be tolerant.

  agent step 1: content: Ligand 1 is a fused-donor-ring molecule, the class that has scored 0.58, 0.58 and 0.59 against plain phenyl's 0.70, so the edit is to unfuse it and put a para substituent on - the only arrangement that has beaten bare phenyl.

Which substituent is worth one of the last few offspring. Nine para cases are measured and eight of them fall between 0.70 and 0.75; exactly one reached 0.77, a nitrile. The activity term is a fingerprint model, so it responds to substructures it has seen in known actives rather than to bulk properties, and that is consistent with what I have observed: no ordering by electronics, no ordering by hydrogen bonding, but one specific group standing slightly apart. If that reading is right, the nitrile's small lead is about the nitrile itself being a recognised motif, not about any property I can interpolate.

The clean test is its isostere. replace_substructure at the bond from atom 9 (the donor N-H, kept) to atom 10 (the benzodioxole, discarded), attaching 4-ethynylphenyl. An ethynyl is the same linear two-atom substituent with the same geometry and nearly the same length as a nitrile, differing only in that the terminal nitrogen becomes a carbon with a hydrogen. Same shape, same rigidity, similar lipophilicity, no lone pair and no dipole.

If it also reaches 0.77, the lead is geometric and the para position rewards a linear rigid substituent. If it falls back to the 0.70 to 0.74 pack, the nitrogen is doing the work and the effect is substructural recognition rather than shape - which would explain why three successive property-based explanations of this position failed.

Expect MW near 271, TPSA 38, three rotatable bonds, QED around 0.78, SA near 1.9 since terminal alkynes are priced above nitriles.

What this molecule buys instead is the answer to why the nitrile is alone at 0.77.

If it reaches 0.77 too, the lead is geometric and the position rewards linear rigid substituents, which is a rule a future run could act on. If it drops to the pack, the nitrogen carries it, the effect is the fingerprint model recognising a specific motif rather than any property, and that explains cleanly why three successive property-based theories of this position - alerts, electronics, hydrogen bonding - each fitted the molecules in front of me and failed on the next one.

That distinction is worth more than a lottery ticket at this stage of the budget. FINAL ANSWER

That question is whether the pocket ring has to be directly bonded to the hinge. Every molecule that has scored above 0.7 has the pocket phenyl joined to pyrimidine C4 by a single bond between two aromatic carbons, which holds the two rings close to coplanar. I have never tested whether that matters, and the pocket's behaviour so far is a set of refusals - a thiophene, two fused bicycles - that I have been explaining as a requirement for benzene specifically. An equally good explanation is that it is a requirement for a flat, directly-conjugated ring, and those two make opposite predictions about what to try next.

The test is to insert a single methylene between the hinge and the pocket, turning the biaryl into a benzyl. The ring is still an unsubstituted benzene, the atom count barely changes, but the conjugation is broken and the ring can rotate freely out of plane. Ligand 1 at 0.74 is the matched reference.

One caveat I will state up front: an analogous test on the donor side earlier in this run showed almost no effect, but that molecule's binding term was 0.01 either way, so it measured nothing. On a 0.74 parent this one will actually resolve.

  agent step 3: content: Reverted to ligand 1 at QED 0.7657, SA 1.6644, binding 0.74.

Atom 10 is pyrimidine C4, the hinge carbon carrying the pocket, and atom 11 is the pocket ring's ipso carbon. replace_substructure at that bond, keeping atom 10 and discarding the phenyl, attaching Cc1ccccc1 - whose first written atom is the methylene, so the ring comes back through a CH2 rather than bonded directly.

The product is ligand 1 with one carbon inserted between hinge and pocket. The pocket is still an unsubstituted benzene, still six aromatic carbons, and the molecule gains 14 Da and one rotatable bond. What it loses is conjugation: the biaryl bond that held the two rings near coplanar becomes an sp3 hinge the ring can rotate freely around.

That separates two explanations of the pocket's behaviour which have been indistinguishable all run. The pocket has refused a thiophene and two fused bicycles, and I have read that as a requirement for benzene specifically. But every one of those experiments replaced the ring itself, and a requirement for a flat, directly-conjugated aromatic would have produced exactly the same three failures. If binding survives near 0.74 the pocket wants a benzene and does not care how it is attached, and future work can use flexible linkers to reach further into the site. If it collapses, the requirement is coplanarity and conjugation, the three earlier failures are one phenomenon rather than three, and the pocket should be treated as a rigid fixed feature.

I expect a poor score - near 1.7 if binding collapses - and I am spending the offspring on it because it is the difference between a scaffold with one rigid pocket and one with a usable vector. Expect MW about 296, four rotatable bonds, QED near 0.75, SA near 1.75.

It was a LogP rule in disguise, because every small substituent I had tried happened to be lipophilic, and the correlation held right up until I tried something that broke it. A morpholine at 332 Da and a methylpiperazine at 345 both score above every halogen and alkyl I measured in the supposedly ideal window, because both drop LogP by a unit.   agent step 1: content: Ligand 1 leads the pool at 2.465 and its distinguishing feature is a single group: a nitrile bonded directly to the donor ring, para to the donor nitrogen. Fifteen other substituents at that position have landed between 0.70 and 0.75; the nitrile alone reaches 0.77. No property orders that set - the runners-up are a strongly donating amine and a chlorine, the bottom is a strongly donating methoxy - and the activity model is a fingerprint model trained on known actives, so the likeliest explanation is that a para-cyanoanilino group is a motif it recognises as a unit.

That hypothesis makes a testable prediction, and it is the cheapest remaining test. If the nitrile is recognised as part of an arrangement, then moving it one bond off the ring should break it, because the aromatic carbon it is attached to is part of what the fingerprint encodes. If instead the nitrile is contributing through its own properties - dipole, polarisability, acceptor character - all of those survive the insertion of a methylene nearly intact.

This matters for the brief more than for the score. A collapse to the 0.70 pack says the motif is specific and must be directly attached - concrete, actionable, and it explains why every property-based account of this position failed.

The comparison is as tight as it can be made. The leader carries a nitrile bonded directly to the donor ring; this carries the same nitrile one methylene further out. Every property the nitrile contributes through - its dipole, its polarisability, its acceptor character, its linear shape - survives that insertion largely intact. What does not survive is conjugation with the ring, and with it the specific aromatic-carbon-plus-nitrile substructure a fingerprint would encode.

So the two explanations of the leader's 0.77 come apart here. If this holds near 0.77, the nitrile works through its properties, and the fact that fifteen other substituents did not reach it needs a different account. If it falls into the 0.70 pack, the motif has to be directly attached, which is concrete and actionable - and it would explain cleanly why alerts, electronics and hydrogen bonding each fitted the substituents in front of me and broke on the next one. A fingerprint model recognising a specific arrangement is not a property gradient and cannot be interpolated, which is what I spent two generations trying to do.

Taken with the five canonical fragments fielded this generation, that would leave the position characterised by two statements rather than a curve: it tolerates nearly anything between 0.70 and 0.77, and the way past 0.75 is a recognised substructure, not a better property. FINAL ANSWER

QED 0.763 against my predicted 0.77, SA 1.9241, MW 346.43, LogP 3.7194, five rotatable bonds. Visible sum about 1.660 against the leader's 1.686, so it needs roughly 0.85 to lead.

It is the linkage half of the recognition question. The morpholine is untouched - same ring, same oxygen, same nitrogen - and only its attachment changes, from bonded directly to the donor ring to bonded through a methylene. That breaks the nitrogen's conjugation with the ring and splits the aryl-amine bond that a fingerprint would encode as one feature.

Together with the cyanomethyl compound from last generation, which does the same thing to the leader's nitrile, this gives two independent readings on the two substituents that have actually mattered. And I already have one point on the other axis: a methylpiperazine, differing from morpholine only at the ring's far position, lost at least 0.08. So if these two also collapse, the rule is that recognition needs the exact fragment bonded directly to the donor ring, and neither its interior nor its linkage can be varied - narrow, but precise and actionable. If they hold, substituents can be carried on a linker and there is far more room than that.

Either answer is worth more to me than another draw.   agent step 1: content: Three episodes left, and the one thing worth mapping is the morpholine that took the pool lead at 0.82 while sixteen other substituents sat between 0.70 and 0.77.

The probes in flight cover composition, size and linker length: a methylpiperazine below 0.74, a piperidine and a pyrrolidine pending, a seven-membered oxazepane pending, and the whole morpholine moved one methylene off the ring. What none of them test is the bond type at the attachment. Every one keeps a nitrogen joined directly to the donor ring, which means they all preserve an N-aryl amine - a feature a fingerprint would encode strongly in its own right, quite apart from the oxygen or the ring.

replace_substructure at the bond from atom 4 (the donor ring carbon, kept) to atom 1 (the sulfonyl sulfur, discarded with both oxygens and its methyl), attaching tetrahydropyran-4-yl through its carbon.

That gives the same six-membered oxygen-containing saturated ring, the same size, the same single heteroatom, but bonded through carbon rather than nitrogen - so the ring's oxygen sits where the morpholine's oxygen sits relative to the attachment, and the aryl-amine bond is gone.

If it scores well, the oxygen-bearing ring is what is recognised and the linkage atom is incidental, which widens the family considerably. If it collapses, the N-aryl bond is part of the motif - and combined with the methylpiperazine's loss to a single interior atom swap, the honest conclusion becomes that the model recognises the para-morpholinoanilino group as a specific unit rather than as an instance of anything describable.

Expect about 331 Da, LogP near 4.2, QED near 0.77, SA near 2.0 - a visible sum around 1.66, needing roughly 0.85 to lead.

Thiomorpholine is morpholine with sulfur in place of that oxygen: same six-membered ring, same N-aryl attachment, same 1,4-relationship, one atom swapped for its heavier congener. It is the sharpest test left of what the oxygen is doing. The two outcomes point opposite ways for whoever runs this next. Sulfur working means the requirement is a heteroatom with a lone pair at that position, and there is a real family to search - thiomorpholines, sulfones, oxygen-bearing rings of various sizes. Sulfur failing means the model recognises the morpholine itself, and the instruction is to install a para-morpholino aniline and spend the budget on parts of the molecule this run never reached.

I expect it to fail, and the reason is worth stating because it cuts against the one property trend this run did find. The halogens rose monotonically with polarisability - fluorine 0.72, chlorine 0.74, bromine 0.75 - so if this position simply rewarded polarisable atoms, sulfur should beat oxygen here. If instead it loses by 0.08, that is a direct argument that substructure recognition, not any atomic property, is what governs this position. FINAL ANSWER

SA 2.8188 against my predicted 2.0, the largest miss of the run on that term, and the cause is something I should have seen before proposing it: 2,6-dimethylmorpholine has two stereocentres. Stereocentres are the single most expensive feature in this accessibility model - it is why the original pool's charged piperidinium sat at 4.24 and why removing two of them in one cut was the biggest SA gain I made all run. I have known that since the first generation and still specified a fragment with two of them without noticing. QED 0.7422, visible sum about 1.540, well behind the leader's 1.686.

I am fielding it rather than undoing, because the binding term is unaffected by SA and the measurement is the only reason the molecule exists. It is the first probe of the morpholine's periphery rather than its core. Azetidine, dimethylamino and methylpiperazine all changed the core and all landed at 0.73 or below; nothing yet tells me whether the carbons flanking the oxygen are part of what is recognised.

If the binding holds near 0.82 with two methyls added there, the recognised unit is the morpholine's nitrogen-oxygen core and the periphery is free - which would be the most actionable rule this run could produce, since it opens a family of substituted morpholines. If it drops to 0.73 with everything else, then the whole fragment is recognised as a unit and the instruction is simply to install an unsubstituted morpholine.

What I would take from the SA miss: I was reasoning about provenance and stopped checking the structural cost. Both matter, and the fragment library I was drawing on has stereocentres scattered through it. FINAL ANSWER

QED 0.7495 against my predicted 0.72 - higher, as I suspected it might be, and for the reason established over the last several episodes: LogP came in at 3.122, the lowest of anything I have built except the aminopyridine, and on this scaffold QED tracks lipophilicity far more than weight. Read across those five, three currently indistinguishable explanations come apart. If only oxygen works, the model recognises the morpholine itself. If sulfur works too but the sulfone does not, what matters is a bare lone pair at that position. If the sulfone works, an acceptor of any kind will do, and the family is wide - which would matter, because the sulfone is the member that also drags LogP down a full unit, and LogP is what has been capping QED on every strong molecule this run.

With one or two episodes of budget left, that series plus the positional isomer from last episode is what I would want a later run to start from: not the leading molecule alone, but the map of which single changes to it are survivable and which are not. FINAL ANSWER

Fluorine and methyl were each mapped across ortho, meta and para, establishing that para is the good position - but both are ordinary substituents with nothing special about them. Sixteen substituents were mapped at para, establishing that the morpholine alone clears 0.77 - but it was only ever tried there. So the pool has never said whether the 0.82 needs the fragment, the position, or both.

Holding near 0.82 would mean recognition dominates geometry: the positional penalty applies only to unremarkable substituents, and a later run should search fragments first and place them second. Collapsing toward 0.50 would mean the two are conjunctive, and the rule is fully specified with nothing negotiable - a para-morpholino aniline, exactly.

I expect the collapse, because every attempt this run to generalise the morpholine by changing one thing has fallen back to the pack, and position is one more thing to change. But if I am wrong it is the most consequential thing in the run, which is why it is worth the second-to-last offspring rather than another draw at a position sampled seventeen times. FINAL ANSWER
